People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Then I miss stuff I really should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these problems with focusing at work are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been stressing me out. Work’s gotten to the point where missed follow-ups and messy coordination on site got my employer formally concerned. And at home… my wife’s pretty worn down having to keep reminding me about things I said I’d handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing at home as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I guess. That’s where I notice it most — like in briefings or plan reviews, I’m trying to pay attention and then a few minutes later my head’s already on other job stuff, and I realize I missed something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that sometimes. I’ll think I know where someone’s going with a question and jump in too fast. Then they finish and I realize I missed the main part.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to that. I mean, I probably shift around some, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively, more than others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. If it’s about a job problem, I can get going and keep explaining it from every angle. Sometimes I can tell the guys are ready to move on and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess in conversations I do. I’ll cut someone off because I think I know what they’re asking, and I answer too quick. Then sometimes I realize they hadn’t gotten to the important part yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects that you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That happens. Like after a site walk, if someone gives me a list of follow-ups, I’ll get the first one or two done, then something else comes up and… later I realize I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed the issue out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Waiting on a call, or at the permit office, even just in line, it gets under my skin. I’m checking the time, shifting around… trying not to push ahead, basically.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve been having trouble staying locked in on things at work. Like I’ll start a safety briefing or go over a plan review trying to pay attention, and a few minutes in my mind’s already on three other job problems. Then I miss stuff I really should’ve caught.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these problems with focusing at work are causing you a lot of stress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been stressing me out. Work’s gotten to the point where missed follow-ups and messy coordination on site got my employer formally concerned. And at home… my wife’s pretty worn down having to keep reminding me about things I said I’d handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble focusing at home as well, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I guess. That’s where I notice it most — like in briefings or plan reviews, I’m trying to pay attention and then a few minutes later my head’s already on other job stuff, and I realize I missed something.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others or blurting out answers before they finish speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do that sometimes. I’ll think I know where someone’s going with a question and jump in too fast. Then they finish and I realize I missed the main part.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to that. I mean, I probably shift around some, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed it as a big thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively, more than others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. If it’s about a job problem, I can get going and keep explaining it from every angle. Sometimes I can tell the guys are ready to move on and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess in conversations I do. I’ll cut someone off because I think I know what they’re asking, and I answer too quick. Then sometimes I realize they hadn’t gotten to the important part yet.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble completing tasks or projects that you've started?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That happens. Like after a site walk, if someone gives me a list of follow-ups, I’ll get the first one or two done, then something else comes up and… later I realize I never sent the email or called the subcontractor or closed the issue out.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty waiting for your turn?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Waiting on a call, or at the permit office, even just in line, it gets under my skin. I’m checking the time, shifting around… trying not to push ahead, basically.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and impulsivity have been present since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, even in elementary school, teachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, talked out of turn, lost homework… stuff like that. I’d rush through directions before I really understood them too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in activities quietly?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t usually think of it that way. I guess I can get kind of antsy, but… I don’t know. Depends what I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your level of focus or impulsivity as you've gotten older?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been worse the last couple years, I’d say. Maybe a little over two years. I mean, it’s always been kind of there, but it’s been seriously getting in the way at work for… about two years and a couple months, since the schedule got more chaotic.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least a few years as an adult where you didn't struggle with focus or impulsivity?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. It’s been there off and on, but it’s really been causing problems at work for about two years and a couple months, since the schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally experienced these difficulties with focus and impulsivity in your personal relationships and daily life outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s not just work. Work’s where it’s gotten me in real trouble — missed follow-ups, site stuff not coordinated right, my employer’s raised concerns. I mean, mostly I notice it at work and at home. Not that I know of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
